# CAMERA V2 — GOVERNANCE

Verbatim copy of Sections 2, 3 and 4 of WORK-ORDER.md. If this file and the work order ever disagree, the work order wins.  
Owner: John Carr · Architect: Claude · Executor: Freebuff Read this, with WORK-ORDER.md and LEDGER.md, at the start of every session.  
---

## 2\. THE HARD LIMITS

| Limit | Value |
| :---- | :---- |
| Live time per Day | 120 minutes. Cannot be exceeded. |
| Spoken script per Day | 10,500 words max (84 min at 125 wpm) |
| Interaction per Day | 36 minutes min (breaks, exercises, discussion, Q\&A) |
| Whole program | 52,500 spoken words max, 10 hours live |
| Chapter tolerance | Each chapter within \+3% of its allowance. Never over the Day total. |

Nobody may "absorb an overage" into another chapter or another Day. That is exactly how Cohort 1 ended up at 119:46 of pure speech with no room for exercises.  
---

## 3\. GOVERNANCE RULES (READ AT THE START OF EVERY SESSION)

Before doing anything, read these three files in this order: this work order, GOVERNANCE.md, LEDGER.md. At the end of every session, update LEDGER.md. (Create GOVERNANCE.md and LEDGER.md in Phase A. GOVERNANCE.md is a copy of Sections 2, 3 and 4 of this file.)  
Budget first, then write. Before drafting a chapter, write its allowance (from Section 4\) at the top of the file. Draft to that number. Do not draft long and trim afterward.  
Count with a program, never by eye. Earlier counts disagreed (2,717 vs 2,693 for the same chapter). Use a word-count program on the final file.  
Count only the words inside the quotation marks after each "Say:".  
Do not count "On screen," "Purpose," or "Cue" lines.  
A hyphenated word counts as one word.  
Minutes \= words divided by 125\.  
Every chapter ends with a Chapter Report (format in Section 5). If any check fails, fix it and re-report. Do not move on.  
One chapter per pass. Do not batch chapters. After each chapter, stop and wait for John's "approved."  
Cut order when over budget (cut in this sequence, stop as soon as you are under): a. repeated restatements of a point already made b. second and third examples (keep the best one) c. lists longer than three items (keep three) d. transitions and throat-clearing e. last resort: mark a subsection PRE-WORK (rule 6\)  
Never cut: a subsection's core claim, an exercise instruction, a chapter's closing lines.  
Pre-work, not deletion. If a subsection cannot fit, it does not vanish. It is marked PRE-WORK in the coverage map, with the book section reference, and it goes in the Pre-Work Pack (reading the attendee does before the Day). Cap: PRE-WORK may not exceed 20% of a Day's source words. If you need more than that, STOP and tell John.  
Every TOC subsection is either spoken (at least one sentence of its core claim) or marked PRE-WORK. The coverage map must show which.  
Promises must be true. If a slide promises something the program no longer delivers, fix the slide.  
No invented numbers. Any figure not from the book is labeled "Illustrative" on screen and in the script.  
Headers must match reality. Slide count, word count, minutes and subsection count in the header must equal what the file actually contains.  
Never claim "verified" without showing the numbers. Show the per-slide table.  
If anything is unclear, ask John in plain text. Do not guess and do not build on a guess.  
---

Formula for any chapter: allowance \= 10,500 × (chapter source words ÷ that Day's source words).

## Freebuff's Phase B correction to the Day 1 "Currently scripted" column

The work order lists Chapter 1 as 2,693 while the old script header declared 2,717. The work order was right and the header was wrong. A standalone em dash is not a word, and the old scripts counted every one of them as a word. Chapter 1's spoken blockquotes contain 2,693 words plus exactly 24 standalone em dashes — which is precisely the 2,717 the old header declared.  
Counted properly, per Section 3's rule (the words inside the quotation marks), the five Day 1 chapters are:

| Chapter | Old header declared | Correct count | Em dashes wrongly counted |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | 2,717 | 2,693 | 24 |
| 2 | 2,341 | 2,329 | 12 |
| 4 | 3,270 | 3,243 | 27 |
| 5 | 3,466 | 3,445 | 21 |
| 6 | 3,176 | 3,152 | 24 |
| Total | 14,970 | 14,862 | 108 |

The corrected Day 1 total is 14,862 words \= 118:54, not 14,970. The work order's own total of 14,946 is a mix: its Chapter 1 figure of 2,693 is correct, while its other four (2,341 / 3,270 / 3,466 / 3,176) are the inflated ones.  
No allowance changes. Allowances are computed from source words, not script words, so the formula in Section 4 is untouched by this. The lesson is recorded because it is the second time a word count has been wrong in this project, and Section 3 warns about exactly that.  
---

## What the counter does with a symbol — and the one place it decides a figure (13-10-26)

The rule above — a standalone em dash is not a word — is the rule the counter applies, and a Day 5 re-measurement on 13-10-26 confirmed it does. That pass also found the only other standalone symbol token sitting inside a measured region anywhere in the book: an arrow (→) at line 12135, inside Chapter 56, on the publishing-cadence line. The counter counts it as a word, exactly as it once counted em dashes.  
This is one word, and it is recorded rather than repaired because of where it lands. On the tabled basis Chapter 56's corrected source is 1,983; with arrows excluded like em dashes it would be 1,982. The formula then gives 10,500 × 1,983 ÷ 16,919 \= 1,230.66 → \*\*1,231\*\* and 10,500 × 1,982 ÷ 16,919 \= 1,230.04 → \*\*1,230\*\* — the two straddle the rounding boundary exactly, so this token is worth exactly one word of Chapter 56's allowance and cannot be worth less. The issued figure is 1,231 and stands, Chapter 56 being unwritten and the ruling applied against it on 13-10-26; if the rule is ever extended to arrows, one word moves and nothing else does. No other Day is affected, because no other measured region in the book contains an arrow token — 74 more arrows exist in the document, in headings, front matter and unscripted material outside every measured region.  
The check is reproducible: count the whitespace tokens on the non-heading lines of a chapter's region, then subtract the standalone em dashes and arrows. That is the instrument the source columns of Days 2, 3, 4 and 5 were measured with.

# CAMERA — SLIDE ARCHITECTURE STANDARD (executive density)

Owner: John Carr · Executor: Freebuff · Created: 11-10-26 Status: applies to every chapter visual specification from Day 2, Chapter 10 onward, and retro-fitted on 11-10-26 to Day 2's Chapters 7, 8 and 9 and to all five Day 1 chapters. Relation to Section 8 of the work order: this document extends the Visual Standard; it does not replace or contradict it. Every rule in Section 8 still holds — one self-contained HTML file per chapter, 1920 × 1080 stage, three colours maximum, one accent, serif headlines, no clip art, no emoji, no neon, no bounce, reduced-motion support, stable end states, and the Illustrative label on any figure that is not from the book. Where this document is stricter than Section 8, Section 8 is the floor and this is the target.  
---

## 1\. THE PROBLEM THIS FIXES

The first generation of slide specifications described one idea and one picture — a heading, a caption, and two or three items. John's note on 11-10-26: "they seem to be all headings and a couple bullet points." He is right, and it is a specification problem before it is a design problem. A slide built from a one-line brief can only ever be a one-line slide.  
The audience is executives and owners of \$10M–30M enterprises. They read decks for a living and they discount anything that looks like a template. What earns their attention is not more decoration. It is structure, evidence and consequence on the same surface: a claim they have not made themselves, the instrument that proves it, and the decision it forces. Restraint is still the visual language — density comes from what the slide knows, not from what it decorates.

## 2\. SLIDE ANATOMY — THE FIVE LAYERS

Every slide in every chapter specification is now written as these five layers, in this order, and the On screen line in the prompter must name all five.

| \# | Layer | What it is | Length |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | Eyebrow | The chapter and the position, in the chrome: CAMERA · DAY 2 · ENGAGEMENT · 04 / 13\. Tells the room where it is. | one line, 32 px |
| 2 | Claim | The slide's argument as a full sentence, in serif, set large. Not a topic label — an assertion. "Attention is a finite budget, and you are bidding for it twice." | 8–16 words |
| 3 | Structure | The instrument: a named framework, matrix, ladder, ledger, timeline, gate or flow. This is the layer that replaces bullets, and it is never a bullet list. | 4–9 labelled cells |
| 4 | Evidence | The data strip or annotated detail that makes the structure credible: counts, the room's own numbers, a worked example, a quoted source, a labelled axis. Every figure carries provenance. | 1–3 items |
| 5 | Consequence | The rule line, in the accent colour, at the foot of the content area: what the business should now do, stop doing, or decide. | 10–18 words |

The label is written one way in every file. On the On screen line and in the Phase D layer list, layer 1 is written chrome — chrome CAMERA · DAY 2 · ENGAGEMENT · 04 / 13 — because what the layer shows is the chrome that carries the chapter and the position. Eyebrow is this table's name for that layer; the two words name one object, and from Chapter 7 onward the label is written as chrome in every file so the programme reads as a single deck.  
The five field labels, in this order and these words. Every new or retro-fitted specification writes the On screen line this way, so any chapter can be read out of any file:  
textCopy  
\*\*\[0:00-0:59\] · On screen:\*\* Chrome \`CAMERA · DAY 1 · C: CHARACTER · 01 / 19\`. Claim, serif: \*…\* Structure: a \*\*comparison ledger\*\* — … Evidence: … , sourced \*Source: CAMERA Ch. 4 §4.2\*. Consequence, accent: \*…\*  
The lane is the part the chapter's slides sit in, named as the book names it. The lettered parts keep their letter — C: CHARACTER, A: AUDIENCE, M: MONETIZATION, E: ENGAGEMENT, R: REPURPOSING — and the Day 1 chapters, which precede them, carry their part's own name: CAMERA SYSTEM FOUNDATION (Chapter 1, Part I) and THE CAMERA OPERATING MODEL (Chapter 2, Part II). TT in 0N / TT is the chapter's slide count.  
The Phase D entry. Each slide's specification entry carries, in this order: Type (the visual in a few words), Build (the step count), Component (the named instrument from the library above), Layers (the five layers, semicolon-separated, ending with the consequence line), What changes (the demonstration, step by step), and Why (the claim that makes it necessary).  
A supplementary instrument is not a slide. It sits beside the deck as the section's own instrument — a worksheet, a sort, an audit — so the five-layer rule and the consequence line do not bind it. Where a specification does give its supplementary a Component and Layers, the list describes the one thing it shows and the presenter's action on it; it does not trail a consequence rule.  
Plus the persistent chrome on every slide: chapter rail, CAMERA wordmark, slide number, and a footer line naming the Day. Nothing else is decorative.  
Density floor. A slide that cannot fill layers 3 and 4 is not a slide. It is either merged with its neighbour or it becomes the supplementary instrument for that section. No slide in any chapter specification may be specified as fewer than two structural cells and one evidence element.

## 3\. THE COMPONENT LIBRARY

Specifications must name the component from this list (a new component may be added, but it goes in this file first so the whole programme stays one system).

| Component | Use it when the claim is about | How it is built |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 2 × 2 matrix | Two variables deciding an outcome | Four quadrants, both axes labelled with their poles, the business's position marked |
| Ladder / stack | Sequence, maturity, or cost as you climb | 3–5 rungs, each labelled, the rung that matters lit |
| Timeline with milestones | Order in time, or decay | A rule with 3–5 dated or staged markers, the turn marked in accent |
| Comparison ledger | Two options, two voices, two versions | Two columns, matched rows, the deciding row kept lit |
| Cost ledger | What something actually costs | Line items with a running total: hours, stages, iterations |
| Gate / flow | A decision with a pass and a fail | Input, gate with its rule, two exits — one of which visibly fails |
| Annotated chart | A quantity moving | Axis labels, the curve, and 2–3 annotations on the curve rather than in a legend |
| Evidence wall | Proof accumulating | Documented items landing one at a time into a grid, empty cells visible |
| Scorecard | Readiness or coverage | Named rows, marked columns, the aggregate held visible |
| Quote panel | A source's own words | The line, the attribution, one rule above it |
| Decision bench | Choosing between options | Options side by side, criteria as rows, the winner not privileged until the last step |
| Workshop frame | Handing the room over | One instruction, the deliverable named, the time on screen |

## 4\. DATA AND PROVENANCE RULES

1. No invented numbers. Unchanged from the work order. A figure that is not from the book, or from the room's own work, is labelled Illustrative on screen in at least 32 px type.  
2. Every figure carries provenance on the slide. The footer of the content area names where it came from: Source: CAMERA Ch. 10 · From your Chapter 8 map · Illustrative — not a measured figure.  
3. Structure over statistics. Where the book contains no figure, the slide carries a structure — a matrix, ladder, ledger or gate — rather than a fabricated chart. Density must never be bought with a number the programme cannot stand behind.  
4. The viewer's own numbers are the best evidence. Where an exercise produces a count (items banked, pillars chosen, pieces per month, stages owned), the slide shows that count and nothing else.  
5. No false precision. No percentages, decimal places or index values that the source does not contain.

## 5\. TYPE, COLOUR AND ZOOM SAFETY

* Ground: deep navy-black. Text: off-white. Secondary: quiet steel. Accent: muted gold. Three colours maximum on a slide, one of them the accent.  
* Serif for the claim, the frame labels and the figure that matters. Clean sans for everything else.  
* Body text at least 44 px. Labels, captions and provenance at least 32 px. (This is a correction: the round-2 style-lock prototype sets labels and chrome at 24 px, 28 px and 30 px — its Illustrative tag is 24 px, its Cost to date labels 28 px, and its kicker, footer, pane tags and pool heading 30 px — all below the Section 8 floor. Those sizes are fixed in every new build — no label below 32 px, ever.)  
* Lines at least 3 px. No hairlines. High contrast only.  
* An 8% safe margin on all edges. The content area is the frame inside it.  
* One thing moves at a time. Nothing moves while the presenter is making the key point.  
* Generous empty space is part of the density: a crowded slide reads as cheap. Density is layers, not ink.

## 6\. MOTION

Unchanged in intent, now specified per layer:

* The structure assembles one component at a time, one keypress per build step. Each component ends in a stable state.  
* The evidence layer lands last in each step that it belongs to, never animating on its own.  
* The consequence line arrives on the final step of the slide and stays.  
* Every demonstration must show something changing that could not be shown statically — a divergence, a comparison failing, a total climbing, a gate refusing. A labelled diagram is not a demonstration.  
* Reduced motion shows every layer in its final state, with transitions at zero.

## 7\. WHY THIS IS "MORE IMPRESSIVE"

Not because it is busier. Because of four things an executive notices immediately:

1. The headline is an argument, not a topic. "Attention" is a topic. "Attention is a finite budget, and you are bidding for it twice" is a position they can disagree with — which is what makes them lean in.  
2. There is an instrument on the slide. A matrix, ladder or ledger they can photograph and use in their own business tomorrow.  
3. The numbers are sourced or the structure is honest. Nothing on the slide is invented, and the slide says so. That is rare enough in this room to be memorable.  
4. The slide tells them what to decide. Every slide ends by naming the decision or the discipline, so the deck reads like advice from a peer rather than a lesson from a vendor.

## 8\. WORKED EXAMPLE — THE SAME SUBSECTION, TWO SPECIFICATIONS

Before (0.4 layers). Ch 10, Slide 01\. Type: statement card. What changes: the words Attention is a finite resource arrive. Why: it opens the chapter.  
After (5 layers).

* Eyebrow: CAMERA · DAY 2 · ENGAGEMENT · 01 / 13  
* Claim: Attention is a finite budget, and every minute of your content is a bid against everything else in the feed.  
* Structure: Gate / flow — a left column labelled broadcast: the viewer had few alternatives and a right column labelled feed: the viewer has an unlimited alternative one thumb-flick away; the same 30-second window drawn across both, and a single exit under each.  
* Evidence: Cost ledger beneath, running the same 30 seconds twice — the alternatives available in the room in 1985 versus the alternatives available on the phone in front of them now — marked Illustrative, because the programme has no measured figure for it, with the provenance line naming it as a demonstration rather than a measurement.  
* Consequence: You are not competing with your industry. You are competing with everything else on the screen.  
* Build: 3 steps — the two columns, then the ledger, then the consequence line.

The round-2 style lock sets its labels and chrome at 24 px (Illustrative), 28 px (Cost to date, Capability you own) and 30 px (kicker, footer, pane tags, pool heading); its two SVG node labels measure \~31 px. All are below the Section 8 floor. The density prototype demonstrates the corrected sizes for the five layers, and any re-build of the style lock should adopt them. The style lock's own sizes are recorded here rather than left to be rediscovered, because LEDGER.md already logs the \~31 px node labels as a deliberate trade — and this standard does not permit that trade.  
One thing this standard does not change: no visual is built until the Day's scripts are approved and the Step A look gate is passed. This document specifies; it does not authorise a build.

# AUDIT — MASTER TOC AGAINST THE BOOK BODY, ALL 58 CHAPTERS

Run: 13-10-26 · Source: 06\_CAMERA\_V1\_BOOK\_REPAIRED.md · Tool: tools/audit-toc-body.awk (kept, so the audit is reproducible) Command: awk \-v tocend=2659 \-v bodystart=5432 \-f tools/audit-toc-body.awk 06\_CAMERA\_V1\_BOOK\_REPAIRED.md

## 1\. The question, and the answer

The question was whether any scripted chapter's coverage map is built on a wrong entry set, starting from Chapter 51's headline anomaly.  
No scripted chapter's coverage map is built on a wrong entry set. All 27 chapters written so far (Days 1–5) were checked mechanically against the book: every one names its text-less entries, and every claimed subsection total equals the TOC's own count for that chapter. The Chapter 44 error found and fixed earlier the same day was in the LEDGER, not in a chapter file, and it was the only one of its kind.  
But the audit found five things the programme did not know, in §3–§7 below. The most consequential are the cause of the text-less entries — stated in the manuscript itself — and one chapter of prose inside Chapter 56's measured source region.

## 2\. Method, and why it is title-based

The repaired book has three layers, and only two carry course content:

| Layer | Lines | Role |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| CAMERA V1 — GOVERNANCE DOCTRINE | 1–816 | governing principles, not course content |
| MASTER TABLE OF CONTENTS | 817–2659 | the authority for every subsection number and title |
| MASTER CURRICULUM & LESSON SPECIFICATION | 3404–5427 | a separate curriculum document, excluded by the body's start line |
| MASTER COURSE BOOK (the body) | 5432–12,335 | the book: 58 chapters |

Matching cannot be numeric, and this is the trap the audit had to avoid. The body's early chapters carry their subsections as unnumbered headings — the TOC says 1.1 The Purpose of CAMERA and the body says \#\# \*\*The Purpose of CAMERA\*\* — and Chapter 14's subsections are not headings at all (§6). A number-only comparison reports Chapters 1–6 and 14 as having no body text, which is false.  
Three heading styles exist in the body, and the tool matches all three by normalised title:

1. \#\#\# \*\*N.M Title\*\* — Chapters 7–13 and 15–58 (the repair's changes 5 and 6, CHANGES.md PART 2\)  
2. \#\# \*\*Title\*\* — Chapters 1–6 (unnumbered)  
3. \*\*Title\*\* as a whole-line bold paragraph — Chapter 14's twelve subsections

Two further boundaries matter and are parameters, not assumptions: the TOC scan stops at line 2659, before the workbook part of the TOC, whose Workbook 1 / 2 / 3 listings reuse the 1.1, 2.1 … numbering (63 such entries — a number-only sweep of the whole TOC region would silently merge them into course chapters 1, 2, 3); and the body scan starts at 5432, so the lesson-specification layer is never read as body text.

## 3\. Headline numbers

| Measure | Result |
| :---- | :---- |
| TOC entries across Chapters 1–58 | 770 |
| Entries with an exact body heading | 737 |
| Entries with no body heading under that title | 33 |
| — of those, title paraphrases (content present, heading reworded) | 3 |
| — of those, genuine unmatched entries | 30 |
| — of those 30, matching the authoring skip rule (§4) | 28 |
| — of those 30, not explained by the rule | 2 (§5) |
| Body headings no TOC entry covers ("uncited") | 14 |

## 4\. The cause of the unmatched entries is written in the manuscript

The single most useful finding is that the text-less entries are not damage. They are the result of an instruction left in the book itself, at line 11052, inside the misplaced block (§7):  
"Before writing, check the TOC slice I paste below and skip any subsection whose title contains: Exercise, Worksheet, Workshop, Testing, Practical, Practicum, Assignment, or Self-Assessment — those belong in the separate Workbook, not the Book."  
28 of the 30 unmatched entries carry one of those words in their title. So the book is not missing that material by accident: the authoring rule deliberately reserved it for the Workbook. Two independent pieces of evidence in the same manuscript support the reading — the master TOC's workbook part lists the Workbook's own versions of exactly that material (6.1 Hook Bank, 6.3 Retention Checklist, 7.2 Audio, 7.3 Lighting), and the rule names the Workbook as the destination.  
What this changes for the programme. The programme has been discovering these entries a few at a time and ruling on them one at a time — Day 2's four, Chapter 14's two, Day 3's four Practicals, Day 4's four, Day 5's nine. They are 23 of the 30, every one of them in a scripted chapter, and every one is now correctly recorded in its chapter's coverage map. The remaining seven sit in chapters the programme does not script (§5).  
The ledger's own wording on this is too broad and is corrected with this audit. LEDGER.md said "the book-repair audit found five TOC entries with no text anywhere in the book". Those five (§11.12, §11.13, §11.14, §12.15, §31.11) were the ones falling in the chapters then being scripted. The book-wide figure is 30\.

## 5\. The two unmatched entries the rule does not explain

Neither is missing content, and neither is in a chapter the programme scripts. Both were settled by reading the body:

| Entry | Chapter | Finding |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| §3.1 Stop Starting With the Camera | 3 (not scripted) | Present, without a heading. The chapter's opening paragraphs at lines 6227–6231 teach it — "The first mistake most businesses make … is starting with the camera … That reverses the order of the problem." The TOC gives it an entry; the body folds it into the chapter introduction. |
| §54.4 Lighting Check | 54 (not scripted) | Present under a one-word variant. The body heading at line 11981 reads Lighting Setup where the TOC entry at line 2539 reads Lighting Check, and the surrounding sections (§54.1–§54.9) match one for one. Title drift, not a gap. |

So no TOC entry in this book has content that is absent from the book: every one is either deferred to the Workbook by rule (28), folded into an unheaded chapter opening (1), or present under a reworded heading (1).  
The three "paraphrases" are the same phenomenon in Chapter 2, where the body renames the TOC's The Difference Between X and Y as X and Y Are Not the Same Thing (§2.10, §2.11, §2.12). Day 1's Chapter 2 map claims 12 of 12 and all twelve do have body text — the map is right, and a title-matching reader would see three phantom gaps.

## 6\. A repair gap the record does not mention: Chapter 14

CHANGES.md PART 2, change 5, reads "\*\*N.M Title\*\* → \#\#\# \*\*N.M Title\*\* (Ch 14–58) | 527". Chapter 14's twelve subsections were not numbered in the source, so change 5 had nothing to convert there, and change 6 (which numbered Chapters 7–13's plain titles from the TOC) did not cover it either. Measured on the repaired copy: \#\#\# \*\*14.x headings in the body \= 0; whole-line bold subsection titles \= 12\.  
Chapter 14 is therefore the only chapter in the programme whose subsections are not headings, and DAY-3/DAY-3-CH14-prompter.md already noticed and worked around it — its map says the body "marks its twelve subsections as bold titles (not numbered headings)". The audit confirms that and records it as a repair the record does not claim, for John to decide whether Chapter 14 should be normalised to the same standard as the other 51 chapters.

## 7\. Prose inside a measured source region — Chapter 56

Three sites in the body contain authoring artifacts rather than book prose. Only one sits inside the measured region of a scripted chapter, and it matters because allowances derive from those counts.

| Site | Lines | Words | Whose region |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| editorial line | 8723 | 12 | Chapter 10 (scripted, Day 2\) |
| the misplaced block (§8) | 11049–11079 | 376 | Chapter 39 (not scripted) |
| instructional note | 12116 | 54 | Chapter 56 (scripted, Day 5, not yet written) |

Line 8723 reads "Here's Chapter 11, corrected to end at 11.11 with no exercise/workshop/worksheet content:" — an authoring note, not prose. Line 12116 reads "Keep this section consistent with the Chapter Contract's standing rule that AI is a workflow tool …" — the same kind of note.  
The consequence, measured, and deliberately not applied. Chapter 56's source is tabled at 2,037 words; 54 of them are the instruction at line 12116\. Subtracting them gives 1,983 and moves the Day's source total 16,919 → 16,865 — and because the Day 5 formula is 10,500 × chapter source ÷ Day source, a correction to the denominator moves every allowance, not just this one. Both coherent routes were computed rather than guessed:

| Route | What it does | Result | Problem |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| A — correct Chapter 56 only | allowance from 10,500 × 1,983 ÷ 16,919 as tabled | Chapter 56 1,265 → 1,231 | the nine allowances then sum to 10,466, breaking the convention that the column equals 10,500 exactly |
| B — recompute the column | all nine from 10,500 × source ÷ 16,865, 2 words given back on the last chapter | 1,353 / 699 / 1,574 / 1,009 / 1,017 / 1,207 / 1,021 / 1,387 / 1,233 \= 10,500 | it re-opens three already-written chapters — Chapter 31 1,349→1,353, Chapter 33 696→699, Chapter 43 1,569→1,574 — the budget they were measured against |
| C — no budget change | keep the column; record that Chapter 56's measured source includes 54 words of artifact | no figure moves | Chapter 56's allowance stays marginally overstated |

My recommendation is C, with the defect recorded, unless John prefers B. The Day 4 precedent governs: when that Day's source total was corrected from 17,227 to 17,196 the six recomputed allowances were disclosed beside the issued ones rather than substituted, because the issued figures are the budget the chapters were written to. Route B is defensible precisely because Chapter 56 is not yet written, so nothing is re-opened mid-flight — but it also moves three chapters that are finished, and that trade is John's to make. Nothing has been changed by this audit. Chapter 10's 12-word contamination sits inside a Day that is complete and whose allowances are tabled; it is recorded for the same reason and changes no figure by itself.

## 8\. Chapter 51's anomaly, resolved and renamed

The ledger carried "§51.7 Backup and Archive is duplicated into the wrong chapter (anomaly F, CHANGES.md)". CHANGES.md contains no anomaly letters, and what it documents in PART 3 is wider and more useful than that sentence: the misplaced block at lines 11056–11079, which John instructed be reported and left alone. Measured on the repaired copy, the block contains:

* 11056 — a pasted \#\# \*\*Chapter 51 — Software Workflows\*\* heading  
* 11057–11070 — a pasted TOC slice of Chapter 51's seven subsection headings, §51.1–§51.7  
* 11071–11079 — an empty heading at 11072, the authoring note at 11049–11052 and 11074, and a duplicate of §50.10

So it is not a duplicate of Chapter 51's content. It is a slice of Chapter 51's index — seven headings with no prose under them — pasted into Chapter 39's region during authoring. Chapter 51's real body is intact at 11814 onward, 7 of 7, which the audit confirms independently: Chapter 51 is one of the six clean TOC-to-body matches in Day 5, and the seven headings the audit reports as misfiled in Chapter 39 are exactly this slice. The ledger's description is corrected to say so, and the audit's "uncited: 7" for Chapter 39 is this block and nothing else.

## 9\. What was checked and found clean

* 27 scripted chapters: every text-less entry is named in its coverage map; every claimed total equals the TOC count; no map presents a gap as covered.  
* Chapter 51's mandated set of 7 — all seven headings present in its own body. The block in Chapter 39 does not touch it.  
* Every other chapter's entry set — 737 of 770 TOC entries match exactly, including all 19 of Chapter 29's, all 15 of Chapter 21's and all 14 of Chapter 43's — the three clean chapters of Days 4 and 5\.  
* Uncited body headings, the reverse direction, number 14 in the whole book: 3 in Chapter 2 and 3 in Chapter 6 (inert worksheet frames), the 7 of the misplaced block, and 1 in Chapter 54 (§5). None is taught course content that a map should have carried.

## 10\. Open items this audit puts to John

1. Chapter 56's source and allowance — subtract the 54 words at line 12116, recompute the allowance (1,265 → 1,231) and the Day total (16,919 → 16,865), and re-verify that the nine allowances still sum to 10,500. Chapter 56 is not yet written, so this is the last cheap moment to do it.  
2. Chapter 14's twelve subsections — normalise them to headings like the other 51 chapters, or record the exception permanently.  
3. Chapter 10's 12 words — record or subtract, for consistency with the above. Its Day is complete and its allowances tabled.  
4. The misplaced block — still open since the repair; the audit now describes exactly what is in it.  
5. The ledger's own two overstatements — corrected in place with this audit: the "five entries … anywhere in the book" scope, and the anomaly-F description.

  :root{  
    \--stone:\#070A0E; \--stone-hi:\#101720;

  }  
  html,body{height:100%;margin:0;background:\#000;overflow:hidden;}  
  body{font-family:"Segoe UI",system-ui,-apple-system,Arial,sans-serif;color:var(--ivory);}  
  \#stage{position:fixed;left:50%;top:50%;width:1920px;height:1080px;transform-origin:50% 50%;  
         overflow:hidden;background:var(--stone);-webkit-user-select:none;user-select:none;}

  .material{position:absolute;inset:0;pointer-events:none;}  
  .material .sheen{position:absolute;inset:-10%;  
    background:radial-gradient(120% 90% at 18% 6%, rgba(255,255,255,.055) 0%, rgba(255,255,255,0) 55%),  
               linear-gradient(168deg, var(--stone-hi) 0%, var(--stone) 42%, \#05070A 100%);}  
  .material .vignette{position:absolute;inset:0;  
    background:radial-gradient(120% 105% at 42% 34%, rgba(255,255,255,.028) 0%, rgba(255,255,255,.008) 34%, rgba(0,0,0,.42) 100%);}  
  .material .inlay.top{position:absolute;left:0;right:0;top:86px;height:3px;  
    background:linear-gradient(90deg,var(--gold-deep) 0%,var(--gold) 22%,var(--gold-light) 50%,var(--gold) 78%,var(--gold-deep) 100%);}

  .frame{position:absolute;left:154px;right:154px;top:96px;bottom:96px;display:flex;flex-direction:column;}

  /\* \---------- layer 1: chrome (eyebrow) \---------- \*/  
  .eyebrow{display:flex;justify-content:space-between;align-items:baseline;  
           font-size:var(--label);letter-spacing:.30em;text-transform:uppercase;color:var(--steel);}  
  .eyebrow .mark{color:var(--ivory);letter-spacing:.42em;}

  /\* \---------- layer 2: claim \---------- \*/  
  h1.claim{font-family:Georgia,"Iowan Old Style","Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:400;  
           font-size:var(--claim);line-height:1.14;margin:18px 0 0 0;max-width:1720px;}  
  h1.claim em{font-style:italic;color:var(--gold-light);}

  /\* \---------- layer 3: structure (gate / flow) \---------- \*/  
  .gates{display:flex;gap:44px;margin-top:20px;}  
  .gate{flex:1 1 0;border:3px solid rgba(138,146,156,.30);  
        background:linear-gradient(160deg,rgba(255,255,255,.030) 0%,rgba(255,255,255,0) 62%);  
        padding:18px 22px 16px 22px;}  
  .gate.lit{border-color:rgba(200,167,91,.62);}  
  .gate h2{font-family:Georgia,"Iowan Old Style","Times New Roman",serif;font-weight:400;  
           font-size:44px;margin:0 0 4px 0;}  
  .gate .sub{font-size:var(--label);color:var(--steel);letter-spacing:.06em;margin:0 0 12px 0;}  
  .gate .window{position:relative;height:48px;border:2px solid rgba(138,146,156,.34);}  
  .gate .window .bar{position:absolute;left:0;top:0;bottom:0;width:0%;  
        background:linear-gradient(90deg,rgba(200,167,91,.20) 0%,rgba(200,167,91,.52) 100%);  
        transition:width 1.05s var(--ease);}  
  .gate .window .tick{position:absolute;top:0;bottom:0;width:2px;background:rgba(237,233,226,.62);left:12%;}  
  .gate .window .lab{position:absolute;right:10px;top:50%;transform:translateY(-50%);  
        font-size:var(--label);color:var(--ivory);letter-spacing:.05em;}  
  .gate .exit{margin-top:10px;font-size:var(--label);line-height:1.25;color:var(--ivory);}

  /\* \---------- layer 4: evidence (cost ledger) \---------- \*/  
  .ledger{margin-top:12px;border-top:2px solid rgba(138,146,156,.26);}  
  .lrow{display:flex;align-items:baseline;gap:24px;padding:6px 0;border-bottom:2px solid rgba(138,146,156,.16);}  
  .lrow .k{flex:0 0 700px;font-size:var(--label);color:var(--steel);letter-spacing:.08em;text-transform:uppercase;}  
  .lrow .v{font-family:Georgia,"Iowan Old Style","Times New Roman",serif;font-size:44px;color:var(--ivory);}  
  .lrow .v.g{color:var(--gold-light);}  
  .prov{margin:8px 0 0 0;font-size:var(--label);color:var(--steel);letter-spacing:.10em;text-transform:uppercase;}

  /\* \---------- layer 5: consequence \---------- \*/  
  .consequence{margin-top:12px;height:3px;width:100%;max-width:1360px;  
    background:linear-gradient(90deg,var(--gold-light) 0%,var(--gold) 46%,var(--gold-deep) 100%);}  
  .consequence-line{margin:12px 0 0 0;font-family:Georgia,"Iowan Old Style","Times New Roman",serif;  
    font-size:var(--body);line-height:1.28;color:var(--gold-light);max-width:1720px;}  
  .consequence-note{margin:6px 0 0 0;font-size:var(--label);color:var(--steel);letter-spacing:.06em;max-width:1720px;}

  .footer{position:absolute;left:154px;right:154px;bottom:30px;display:flex;justify-content:space-between;  
          align-items:baseline;font-size:var(--label);letter-spacing:.22em;color:var(--steel);text-transform:uppercase;}  
  .footer .mark{color:var(--ivory);letter-spacing:.42em;}

  .reveal{opacity:0;transform:translateY(12px);}  
  .reveal.live,.live .reveal{opacity:1;transform:translateY(0);transition:opacity .7s var(--ease),transform .7s var(--ease);}  
  html\[data-motion="off"\] \*{transition:none \!important;animation:none \!important;}  
\</style\>  
\</head\>

  \<div class="material"\>  
    \<div class="sheen"\>\</div\>

    \</div\>

    \<\!-- LAYER 3 · STRUCTURE — gate / flow \--\>  
    \<div class="gates"\>  
      \<div class="gate" data-step="1"\>

      \<div class="consequence"\>\</div\>  
      \<p class="consequence-line"\>You are competing with the screen, not with your industry.\</p\>

\<script\>  
(function(){  
  var stage \= document.getElementById('stage');  
  var total \= 3, step \= 0;  
  var motion \= new URLSearchParams(location.search).get('motion') \!== 'off';  
  document.documentElement.setAttribute('data-motion', motion ? 'on' : 'off');  
  if (window.matchMedia && window.matchMedia('(prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)').matches) {  
    motion \= false; document.documentElement.setAttribute('data-motion','off');  
  }

  function fit(){  
    var w \= window.innerWidth || document.documentElement.clientWidth || 1920;  
    var h \= window.innerHeight || document.documentElement.clientHeight || 1080;  
    var s \= Math.min(w/1920, h/1080);  
    stage.style.transform \= 'translate(-50%,-50%) scale(' \+ s \+ ')';  
  }  
  window.addEventListener('resize', fit);  
  window.addEventListener('load', fit);  
  fit(); setTimeout(fit, 80); setTimeout(fit, 400);  
  if (window.ResizeObserver) { new ResizeObserver(fit).observe(document.documentElement); }

  function render(){  
    document.querySelectorAll('\[data-step\]').forEach(function(el){  
      var n \= parseInt(el.getAttribute('data-step'), 10);  
      el.classList.toggle('live', n \<= step);  
    });  
    document.querySelectorAll('.gate').forEach(function(g, i){  
      g.querySelector('.bar').style.width \= (step \>= 1\) ? (i \=== 0 ? '64%' : '18%') : '0%';  
    });  
  }  
  function next(){ if (step \< total) { step++; render(); } }  
  function prev(){ if (step \> 0\) { step--; render(); } }

  document.addEventListener('keydown', function(e){  
    if (e.key \=== ' ' || e.key \=== 'ArrowRight') { e.preventDefault(); next(); }  
    else if (e.key \=== 'ArrowLeft') { e.preventDefault(); prev(); }  
    else if (e.key \=== 'm' || e.key \=== 'M') {  
      var on \= document.documentElement.getAttribute('data-motion') \=== 'on';  
      document.documentElement.setAttribute('data-motion', on ? 'off' : 'on');  
      var el \= stage; el.style.display='none'; void el.offsetHeight; el.style.display='';  
    }  
  });  
  stage.addEventListener('click', function(){ next(); });

  // open at the stable final state, so the whole five-layer slide can be judged at a  
  // glance; the left arrow walks the build backwards from there.  
  step \= total; render();  
  window.\_\_cameraStep \= function(n){ step \= Math.max(0, Math.min(total, n)); render(); return step; };  
})();  
\</script\>  
\</body\>  
\</html\>

### Day 2 — chapters 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13

Measurement method. Source words are counted on the repaired copy with all heading lines excluded, so the heading repair cannot affect the measure. The work order fixes Day 2's source total at 18,206; my heading-free count is 17,886, a 1.8% difference that comes from which heading lines are included in the original tally. Chapter 7 is 1,711 words \= 13:41 against its 1,678 allowance (+2.0%, inside the ceiling), Chapter 8 is 1,917 words \= 15:20 against its 1,931 allowance (14 words under, −0.7%), Chapter 9 is 1,477 words \= 11:49 against its 1,474 allowance (+0.2%), Chapter 10 is 1,500 words \= 12:00 against its 1,472 allowance (+1.9%), Chapter 11 is 1,042 words \= 8:20 against its 1,059 allowance (17 words under, −1.6%), Chapter 12 is 1,355 words \= 10:50 against its 1,375 allowance (20 words under, −1.5%) and Chapter 13 is 1,482 words \= 11:51 against its 1,511 allowance (29 words under, −1.9%). Section 4 fixes Day 5's source total at 17,672; this count is 16,919, a 4.3% difference — wider than Day 4's, Day 3's and Day 2's, but of the same kind. Unlike Day 4's, this one does move two allowances — Chapter 47 a word down and Chapter 56 a word up — and both chapters are unwritten, so the correction is applied rather than merely disclosed here; on the basis measured here the column summed to exactly 10,500 and the ceiling column to 10,811. Superseded the same day by John's ruling on Chapter 56's authoring note — see the ruling below and the footnote above — under which Chapter 56's source is 1,983, its allowance 1,231, and the three columns read 16,865 / 10,466 / 10,776 at 83:43.  
A measuring defect found while building this table, and what it changes. The first version of this table, written earlier in the same session, carried 2,198 / 1,136 / 2,553 / 1,634 / 1,650 / 1,953 / 1,650 / 2,250 / 2,056 \= 17,080 — and those nine figures are exactly the corrected ones plus the standalone em dashes in each chapter (25 / 14 / 25 / 14 / 17 / 14 / 10 / 23 / 19 \= 161, and 16,919 \+ 161 \= 17,080 to the word). Its own prose total was one word low, at 17,079, against the 17,080 its nine rows sum to, and its Chapter 56 row read 2,056 where the corrected count is 2,037 — two slips in the same pass, both corrected on 13-10-26 rather than left standing. In other words the instrument counted a standalone em dash as a word. GOVERNANCE.md already documents this precise error: it is how the Day 1 headers were inflated by 108 words, and the correction recorded there is that "a standalone em dash is not a word." The same instrument was then run back over Day 4 and it reproduces five of that Day's six tabled figures exactly (2,644 / 3,356 / 2,812 / 2,105 / 2,986) — the five chapters that contain no standalone em dashes at all. Chapter 30 is the sixth, and it contains exactly 31 of them: its tabled 3,324 was 3,293 plus 31\. So Day 4's measured source total is 17,196 and Chapter 30's source is 3,293, where its table, its Day Report and the Chapter 19–30 headers had carried 17,227 and 3,324. This is a documentation-only correction: no spoken word, cue span or count moves in any Day 4 file, and the six allowances are retained with their variance disclosed in the Day 4 section above. It was applied on 13-10-26 — Day 4's table, its Day Report and its six chapter headers now carry 17,196 and 3,293 — and it is logged because the discipline is to record a corrected figure, not to leave two instruments in the same ledger. The same pass found and fixed three further slips in these columns: Chapter 30's ceiling, carried as 2,086 where the stated floor rule gives 2,085; Day 4's ceiling total, 10,813 for 10,812; and Day 5's Chapter 56, tabled at 2,036 (16,918 across the Day) where the count is 2,037 (16,919).  
A 54-word authoring note inside Chapter 56's measured source, and the ruling that prices it — applied 13-10-26. The TOC-to-body audit (AUDIT-TOC-BODY-2026-10-13.md §7) found authoring artifacts inside three measured regions, and one of them sits inside a scripted chapter's count: line 12116, inside Chapter 56, carries 54 words addressed to the writer — "Keep this section consistent with the Chapter Contract's standing rule that AI is a workflow tool within your content system, not the subject or centerpiece of it…" — an instruction about how the section is to be written rather than the section itself. Chapter 56's measured size is therefore 1,983 words, not the 2,037 the table carried, and the Day's source total 16,865, not 16,919. Why it was put to John instead of applied: the Day 5 formula is 10,500 × chapter source ÷ Day source, so a corrected denominator moves all nine allowances, and the audit costed both coherent routes before asking — A recomputes Chapter 56 alone against the as-tabled denominator (1,265 → 1,231, and the nine then sum to 10,466, breaking the convention that the column equals 10,500 exactly); B recomputes all nine from the corrected denominator to reach 10,500 exactly, but does it by moving Chapter 31 1,349 → 1,353, Chapter 33 696 → 699 and Chapter 43 1,569 → 1,574 — three chapters already written and judged against the issued figures; C records the defect and moves no figure. John ruled for A on 13-10-26: shrink Chapter 56 alone, and record the consequence. Applied, the column reads 1,349 / 696 / 1,569 / 1,005 / 1,013 / 1,203 / 1,018 / 1,382 / 1,231 \= 10,466, ceilings 1,389 / 716 / 1,616 / 1,035 / 1,043 / 1,239 / 1,048 / 1,423 / 1,267 \= 10,776, planned minutes 648 / 334 / 753 / 482 / 486 / 577 / 489 / 663 / 591 \= 5,023 seconds \= 83:43. Two consequences are recorded rather than smoothed away. First, the nine allowances no longer sum to 10,500 — they sum to 10,466, so 34 words (16 seconds) of the Day are budgeted to no chapter, and the plan closes at 83:43 where it closed at 83:59; the Day's 84:00 ceiling is untouched, so the Day still has its full speaking time and is merely 16 seconds under-planned. Second, the three written chapters route B would have moved are untouched — that is what the ruling bought: Chapter 56 is the last unwritten chapter in the Day, so shrinking it re-opens nobody's accepted work, where re-splitting the Day would have re-opened three. The rejected route was C — record the defect and move no figure — which was the audit's recommendation and mine; the ruling went the other way, and the arithmetic above is what it costs and what it buys. One forward figure is superseded by it and is corrected at its own row: Chapter 44's closing projection "5,881 words \= 47:02 … 10,487 of 10,500 — 13 words of margin" now reads 5,847 \= 46:46 closing at 10,453 — 47 words of margin, with Chapter 43's "6,886 … 10,492" reading 6,852 \= 54:49 closing at 10,458 — 42 words. Chapter 10's 12 words are the same kind of artifact and are recorded without being subtracted — its Day is complete and its allowances are the figures its nine chapters were written and judged against, so the treatment the ruling licenses for the unwritten Chapter 56 is unavailable there. The misplaced 376-word block measures nothing at all, because it sits in Chapter 39, which the programme does not script.

Two findings came out of the verification pass run after the ruling, and both are recorded rather than left for the next reader. The first is the one-second slip corrected at the †/‡ notes above. The second is an instrument inconsistency disclosed and deliberately not acted on: the source counter drops standalone em dashes, as GOVERNANCE.md requires, and it does — but it counts one standalone arrow (→) as a word: line 12135, inside Chapter 56, the only such token inside any measured region in the whole book. So Chapter 56's corrected source is 1,983 on the tabled basis and would be 1,982 if the rule were extended to arrows, which its own rationale supports. The two straddle a rounding boundary exactly — 1,983 × 10,500 ÷ 16,919 \= 1,230.66 gives 1,231, and 1,982 gives 1,230.04 → 1,230 — so this single token is worth one word of Chapter 56's allowance and no more. The ruled figure is 1,231 and stands; GOVERNANCE.md now carries the check, and no other chapter in any Day is affected, because no other measured region contains an arrow.  
Mandated subsections, and the nine text-less TOC entries. §31.1–31.11 (11), §33.1–33.10 (10), §43.1–43.14 (14), §44.1–44.13 (13), §47.1–47.9 (9), §48.1–48.13 (13), §50.1–50.10 (10), §51.1–51.7 (7), §56.1–56.15 (15) \= 101 TOC entries, and Day 5's mandated set is 100 once §31.11 is dropped on John's recorded ruling. (This sentence read "Four chapters" for six listed names; corrected 13-10-26 in the same pass.)

No ruling is now outstanding for any Day 5 chapter. The only Day 5 items still proposed rather than settled are the exercise split and the break placement, and neither changes a word count.

A programme-wide TOC-to-body audit, run 13-10-26, and what it settles. tools/audit-toc-body.awk (kept, so the audit is reproducible) matches the master TOC against the book body, by normalised title rather than by number — necessary because the body's Chapters 1–6 carry unnumbered subsection headings and Chapter 14 carried none at all when the audit ran (corrected 13-10-26: Chapter 14's twelve subsections were normalised to numbered headings that day on John's ruling — CHANGES.md PART 2B — which leaves the tool's title-matching method unaffected and the audit's counts unchanged) — and the full report is AUDIT-TOC-BODY-2026-10-13.md. Headline: 770 TOC entries across Chapters 1–58, 737 with an exact body heading, 33 without — 3 of those title paraphrases and 30 genuine — and 14 body headings that no TOC entry covers. No scripted chapter's coverage map is built on a wrong entry set: all 27 written chapters were checked mechanically, every one names its text-less entries, and every claimed subsection total equals the TOC's own count for that chapter. The Chapter 44 error found earlier the same day was in this ledger, not in a chapter file. The cause of the 30 unmatched entries is written in the manuscript itself — line 11052 carries the authoring instruction to "skip any subsection whose title contains: Exercise, Worksheet, Workshop, Testing, Practical, Practicum, Assignment, or Self-Assessment — those belong in the separate Workbook, not the Book" — and 28 of the 30 match it, so that material is deferred by rule rather than missing. The Workbook part of the master TOC lists its own home (6.1 Hook Bank, 6.3 Retention Checklist, 7.2 Audio, 7.3 Lighting), and that part reuses the N.M numbering for 63 of its entries, which is why any number-only sweep of the whole TOC region merges workbook chapters into course chapters 1, 2 and 3\. The two the rule does not explain are both present in the body: §3.1 Stop Starting With the Camera is taught in Chapter 3's unheaded opening paragraphs, and §54.4 Lighting Check appears as the body heading Lighting Setup at line 11981\. So no TOC entry in this book has content that is absent from the book — 28 deferred to the Workbook by rule, 1 folded into a chapter opening, 1 reworded, 3 paraphrased in Chapter 2\. Two further findings are open items for John. (1) Chapter 14's twelve subsections were still bold paragraphs, not headings — a repair gap CHANGES.md PART 2 did not claim, because change 5's 527 lines were numbered bold titles (Ch 14–58) and Chapter 14's titles were unnumbered, so change 5 had nothing to convert and change 6 covered only Chapters 7–13. The Day 3 Chapter 14 file noticed and worked around this by title; the audit measured it (\#\#\# \*\*14.x headings in the body \= 0, whole-line bold subsection titles \= 12). Ruled by John on 13-10-26: normalise them — done the same session as a second repair pass (CHANGES.md PART 2B), converting the twelve to \#\#\# \*\*14.N Title\*\* from the TOC with tools/normalize-ch14-headings.awk, verified line by line: 24 changed lines, 0 blanked, the TOC region and every line outside 9106–9220 byte-identical, line count unchanged at 12,335, original hash unchanged, copy sha256 now a94af88a…. Counts now read 12 headings and 0 bold titles, and the book stands at 58 of 58 chapters and 1,647 numbered subsection headings. One measured figure follows the repair rather than resisting it: those twelve titles were not heading lines before it, so they were counted in Chapter 14's source, and on the recorded heading-free method its Day 3 re-measurement falls 2,138 → 2,116 with the Day 3 region total 16,936 → 16,914. No allowance moves — Day 3's column is retained in full pending its own open ruling — and no scripted chapter's word count is affected. (2) Two measured source regions contain authoring artifacts, not book prose — 54 words inside Chapter 56's (line 12116\) and 12 words inside Chapter 10's (line 8723), against 376 words in the misplaced block in unscripted Chapter 39\. All three were put to John on 13-10-26 and all three are now settled. Chapter 56 — route A, shrink it alone: its source is corrected to 1,983 and its allowance to 1,231, the column left at 10,466, with the ruling and its full arithmetic recorded in the Day 5 section above. Chapter 10's 12 words — recorded, not subtracted: its Day is complete and its allowances are the figures its chapters were written and judged against, so the treatment the ruling licenses for the unwritten Chapter 56 is unavailable here. The misplaced block — left in the copy, unrepaired and undeleted, in Chapter 39, which the programme does not script, so it measures nothing and moves no figure. It was my recommendation and the audit's to route all three through C, moving no figure; the ruling took the correction for Chapter 56 and the record-only treatment for the other two, and the record carries both the decision and its cost — 34 words of the Day 5 budget unassigned.

## WPM RESCALING RULE (Section 10\)

If John's real speaking time differs from the planned time by more than 8%, recompute the true words-per-minute and rescale the Section 4 budgets before more scripts are written. 8% of 125 wpm is ±10 wpm, so any measured pace outside 115–135 wpm triggers a rescale of every remaining Day.

Day 5 needs no exception, and its margin is the second-widest on the live clock of the five Days: 47 words and 23 seconds, against Day 3's 412 words and 3:18 and ahead of Day 4's 11 seconds and Day 2's 9\. It fits because six of its nine chapters landed on the exact allowance rather than anywhere inside their tolerance, and the three that did not were deliberately under — Chapters 31, 33 and 44 by 1, 7 and 5 words. The Day is also the second Day (after Day 3\) with no chapter over its allowance, and the first in which exactness was the norm rather than the exception.  
The drafting history is the Day's clearest finding and it is recorded in the report. First assemblies ran \+32.3% / \+67.4% / \+22.3% / \+26.2% / \+8.1% / \+24.9% / \+5.7% / \+17.8% / \+22.7% over the nine allowances, and the pattern is not length but subject count: Chapter 50's ten functions missed by 5.7%, Chapter 51's seven routes by 17.8%, Chapter 56's fifteen builder sections by 22.7% and Chapter 48's thirteen configurations by 24.9%, while the three chapters drafted to that rate from the first line landed within one pass. Chapters 50, 51 and 56 each needed one cut pass plus a small restoration; Chapters 43, 47 and 48 needed two, two and four.  
The mid-session break has a home in a cue for the first time in the programme. Days 3 and 4 each reported that no cue in their files placed the mandated break, and Days 1 and 2 place theirs in their Day Reports and in no chapter cue. Day 5's Chapter 33, Slide 11 cue reads "This is the Day's break point — the one cue in Day 5 that does host it. Take five minutes here.", at the end of the Instructor Review that closes the Day's whole interactive block. The placement remains a proposal rather than a ruling, and the two readings still differ: with the break, interaction is 36:00 and the Day passes; break-free it is 31:00, five minutes under the minimum, and a Day FAIL. Day 5 is the cheapest of the five Days to settle it, because ratifying the placement costs no edit — Days 3 and 4 would each need one hand-off cue reworded.  
Three items go to John with this Day, and only one of them has no precedent. (1) The break placement above. (2) Day 5's exercise split — proposed, not settled: all four exercises at five minutes each sit in Chapter 33 (§33.7–§33.10), the Day's whole 20-minute budget in one chapter, replacing the Day 1 default 6 \+ 8 \+ 6\. This is the fourth successive Day with an unsettled exercise decision, and Day 5's is the largest single concentration of interaction in the programme. (3) Chapter 56's substituted provenance — the book's Chapter 56 assumes Chapters 52 through 55 have run, and those four chapters are not in this programme, so the three affected subsections keep their core claims and are grounded in the chapters the Day does deliver, with no slide claiming the cycle was run. Nothing is dropped, nothing becomes PRE-WORK and no word count moves; it is put to John because the substitution should be his to accept rather than the executor's to assume. Day 2's 6 \+ 8 \+ 6 remains unconfirmed and is carried with them.  
Two header documentation gaps were found while assembling the Day's chapter table and both are closed. Chapter 43's header stated its source figure and omitted the compression clause every other Day 5 chapter carries; it now reads 1.61 : 1 on the Day's uniform basis (2,528 ÷ 1,569). Chapter 33's header and its report both read 1.63 : 1, which is source ÷ delivered (1,122 ÷ 689), where the uniform basis is source ÷ allowance (1,122 ÷ 696 \= 1.61); both now read 1.61. This is the same mixed state Day 4's report found in two of its own chapters, settled the same way — uniformly on the allowance basis. No spoken word, cue span, word count, minute, slide count or build-step total moved in either file, verified by re-running the counter and the stamping tool over both: 689 and 1,569 words, both reproducing their spans byte for byte. Day 5's nine headers now all quote 1.61 : 1\.  
What Day 5 adds to the method. Two things earlier Days did not show: exactness is achievable at scale — six chapters landing on the allowance to the word, each cut or restored to the number — and a Day's budget can be smaller than the sum of its allowances and still close cleanly, since John's ruling left 34 words of the Day unassigned and the Day finished 13 words under what remained. Neither would have been possible with a chapter written to tolerance. The five Days now read 4 / 3 / 0 / 1 / 0 chapters over their allowances.

The round-2 build produced three defects that measurements caught and one that only a screenshot caught:

1. The counters were never on screen. In the first round-2 build the cost/capability readouts sat at stage y 1101–1382 — past the 1080 stage — so the whole point of Scene 1 was invisible. DOM-level checks had passed because textContent and the meter's percentage updated correctly off-screen; text in the DOM is not text on the screen. The readouts were moved to a strip below each ring (a ?motion=on screenshot then showed them).  
2. The node plates were clipped. The drawing's plates span x −24…724 inside a 700-unit viewBox, so the left and right plates were cut at the box edge. The viewBox is now cropped to the ink (-26 40 752 620), which both un-clips them and removes 80px of dead margin.  
3. The lap badge was drawn through the headline (182–228 over 154–240). It now sits at the frame's top right, 599 stage px clear of the headline.  
4. Node labels shrank to \~31px as the price of fitting ring \+ readouts \+ note inside the frame (top 86, bottom 994). Recorded here because it is a visible trade, not a defect.

Scene 1 vertical budget rule for future scenes: the .frame is the safe area, so Scene 1's total — header, pane head, ring and readout strip — must fit 196 → 908 inside it. The first round-2 layout needed 1414\.  
Day 1 program counts settled: 2,693 / 2,329 / 3,243 / 3,445 / 3,152 \= 14,862 words \= 118:54 (chapter minutes rounded to the nearest second can differ from the total by a second or two). The old headers' 14,970 counted 108 standalone em dashes as words. The work order's Chapter 1 figure of 2,693 was correct; its total of 14,946 is a mix of one correct and four inflated figures (see the correction note in GOVERNANCE.md). No allowance changes.

# CAMERA V2 — BOOK REPAIR: CHANGES AND FINDINGS

Source of truth (never edited): D:\\\_Downloads\\06\_CAMERA V1 —BOOK FULL.md sha256 of the original, verified before and after all work: 28ee518de26750c90459e079f7c85699d0cd37fa76ca19f4a824c22f154c0447 — unchanged Repaired copy: CAMERA\_V2/06\_CAMERA\_V1\_BOOK\_REPAIRED.md sha256 of the copy, after the first repair pass (13-10-26): b810b96701261f790384e80b118385bffb188eaed7fcdb5996f60500ee5cc7e0 sha256 of the copy, after the second repair pass (13-10-26, PART 2B): a94af88aff8d0bece45048e0ca8afad2c3dc4f9a4ebe5ec66e4de77f243297c1 Repair scripts: CAMERA\_V2/tools/normalize-headings.awk (pass 1\) and CAMERA\_V2/tools/normalize-ch14-headings.awk (pass 2\) — kept so the repair is reproducible and auditable Both files: 12,335 lines. The two passes changed no line's existence and no line's prose — 706 heading lines in pass 1, 12 in pass 2\.  
---

## PART 1 — WHY THIS IS NO LONGER 9 LINES

The work order scoped the repair to "the unmarked chapter headings in Ch 7 to 13". Investigating that finding showed the defect is systemic and that 9 lines does not fix it:

* Chapters 14 to 58 are also not headings. They are bold text (\*\*Chapter 14 — …\*\*), not \# \*\*Chapter 14 — …\*\*. 45 chapters.  
* PART headings are unmarked from PART V onward — 25 part headings in the body.  
* Chapters 7 to 13 have no subsection headings at all — 100 subsections of plain text such as Education vs. Promotion at line 8063, which is §7.1.  
* Chapters 14 to 58 number their subsections but do not mark them as headings — 527 subsections as \*\*14.1 …\*\*.

John's response to the scope question was that he believed the TOC he supplied had already fixed the headings. It had not, and it cannot: the TOC is an index at lines 835–2649 that lists the headings; editing an index does not change the body. What the TOC does provide is the authority for every subsection's number and exact title, which is what makes the repair mechanical rather than guesswork. The repair was therefore extended to the full structural job.  
---

## PART 2 — CHANGES MADE (706 lines)

| \# | Change | Lines | Verification |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | Chapter N — Title → \# \*\*Chapter N — Title\*\* (Ch 7–13) | 7 | plain chapter titles remaining \= 0 |
| 2 | \*\*Chapter N — Title\*\* → \# \*\*Chapter N — Title\*\* (Ch 14–58) | 45 | chapters extractable as headings \= 58 of 58 |
| 3 | \# \*\*Audience Exercise\*\*, \# \*\*Audience Profile Worksheet\*\* → \#\# \*\*…\*\* | 2 | Chapter 6 subsections at \#\# \= 17, matching its TOC |
| 4 | PART R — Title / \*\*PART R — Title\*\* → \# \*\*PART R — Title\*\* | 25 | stray unmarked PART lines remaining \= 0 |
| 5 | \*\*N.M Title\*\* → \#\#\# \*\*N.M Title\*\* (Ch 14–58) | 527 | numbered subsection headings in body |
| 6 | plain subsection title → \#\#\# \*\*N.M Title\*\*, number from the TOC (Ch 7–13) | 100 | per-chapter body counts match TOC exactly |

Total: 706 lines. Titles were never altered — only the markers around them.

### The 100 restored subsection numbers (change 6\)

For every one of Chapters 7–13, the Nth plain title in the chapter body corresponds to §N.x in the TOC. This was verified before use by pairing each body title with the TOC title at the same position: 15/15, 18/18, 15/15, 12/12, 11/11, 14/14, 15/15 matched character-for-character (the only apparent differences were curly versus straight quotation marks in §11.8 and §11.9, which are the same titles). No judgement was required.

### Verification of the whole repair

Audited by comparing the original and the copy line by line (both 12,335 lines):

* changed lines \= 706, every one of them in categories 1–6 above  
* blanked lines \= 0  
* prose lines changed \= 0  
* TOC region, lines 835–2649: byte-identical to the original (verified by direct diff)  
* original sha256 unchanged

The 706 reconcile to the categories exactly, each line classified on its own: 7 plain chapter titles and 7 plain PART headings gained markers and bold, 45 bold chapter titles gained markers, 25 − 7 \= 18 PART headings were already bold, 2 Chapter 6 headings changed level, 527 bold subsection headings gained markers, and 100 plain subsection titles gained a number. That is 7 \+ 45 \+ 2 \+ 25 \+ 527 \+ 100 \= 706\.  
The only two lines whose text was not preserved verbatim. Changes 1–5 alter markers around existing text and change nothing inside it. Change 6 necessarily inserts a section number into title text, and on exactly two of its 100 lines the copy took the TOC's wording rather than the body's: §11.8 line 8797 and §11.9 line 8805 were The "You Are Doing This Wrong" Structure in the body and are now the TOC's The “You Are Doing This Wrong” Structure — the same title with typographic quotation marks. Both lines now byte-match the TOC entries at lines 1227 and 1229, which are the authority for these titles. No prose line anywhere in the document was altered.

### An error I made and caught

My first run of the repair script harvested the TOC with a next statement, which dropped those lines from the output array and blanked 767 TOC entries to empty lines. The line-by-line audit caught it immediately (blanked=767, 767 unapproved changes). I restored the copy from the pristine original, fixed the script, and rebuilt. The final audit above reports blanked=0. The corrected script is the one committed at CAMERA\_V2/tools/normalize-headings.awk. No damaged version of the copy remains on disk.

## PART 2B — SECOND REPAIR PASS: CHAPTER 14 (12 lines)

Raised by AUDIT-TOC-BODY-2026-10-13.md §6, ruled by John on 13-10-26: normalise them.  
Pass 1 had a gap it did not claim. Change 5 converted 527 numbered bold subsection titles (Ch 14–58), and Chapter 14's twelve subsections carried no numbers — so change 5 had nothing to convert there, and change 6 numbered only Chapters 7–13. Chapter 14 was therefore the one chapter in the book whose subsections were not headings at all.

| \# | Change | Lines | Verification |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 7 | \*\*Title\*\* → \#\#\# \*\*14.N Title\*\*, number from the TOC (Ch 14 only) | 12 | \#\#\# \*\*14.x headings in the body \= 12; whole-line bold titles there \= 0 |

Run with CAMERA\_V2/tools/normalize-ch14-headings.awk, which carries the TOC's twelve Chapter 14 entries (§14.1 Vocal Presence to §14.12 Sounding Human) as an ordered list and converts a whole-line bold title only when it equals the Nth entry — inside the Chapter 14 region only, so a same-titled bold line elsewhere cannot match. §14.13 and §14.14 are absent from the list because they carry no heading in the body.  
The pairing needed no judgement: the twelve body titles and the twelve TOC entries matched 1:1, in order, character for character. The repair adds markers and a number around existing text; nothing inside a title was altered, and no line was added, removed or blanked.

* blanked lines \= 0; non-title lines altered \= 0  
* TOC region, lines 835–2649: byte-identical, and every line outside 9106–9220 byte-identical  
* line count unchanged (12,335) — no line's existence altered  
* whole-line bold titles book-wide: 229 → 217, exactly the twelve converted  
* original sha256 unchanged (28ee518d…), verified after this pass

Copy sha256 after both passes: a94af88aff8d0bece45048e0ca8afad2c3dc4f9a4ebe5ec66e4de77f243297c1. With this pass the book has 58 of 58 chapters and 1,647 numbered subsection headings, and Chapter 14 carries §14.1–§14.12 as \#\#\# headings like every other chapter — §14.13 and §14.14 remain text-less by rule and are authored by the Chapter 14 script, which is unchanged by this repair.

## PART 4 — PROGRAM-COUNT FIGURES (Section 3: "count with a program")

The work order flags Chapter 1 as 2,717 versus 2,693 and instructs that the program count be used. Counted with a program, counting only the words on the spoken lines after each Say::

| Chapter | Correct program count | Minutes at 125 wpm | Allowance (Section 4\) | Over |
| :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- | :---- |
| 1 | 2,693 | 21:33 | 1,642 | \+1,051 |
| 2 | 2,329 | 18:38 | 1,906 | \+423 |
| 4 | 3,243 | 25:57 | 2,318 | \+925 |
| 5 | 3,445 | 27:34 | 2,551 | \+894 |
| 6 | 3,152 | 25:13 | 2,083 | \+1,069 |
| Total | 14,862 | 118:54 | 10,500 | \+4,362 |

### The em-dash error — an earlier claim of mine, corrected

In Phase A I reported that "2,717 is the program count for Chapter 1" and that the work order's 2,693 was stale. That was wrong, and the work order was right.  
The old script headers counted every standalone em dash as a word. Chapter 1's spoken blockquotes contain 2,693 words plus exactly 24 standalone em dashes — which is precisely the 2,717 the old header declared. Removing the dashes gives 2,693. The same error runs through the other four chapters (12, 27, 21 and 24 dashes respectively — 108 in total), which is why the old headers totalled 14,970.  
Counted correctly, per Section 3's rule (the words inside the quotation marks), Day 1 is 14,862 words \= 118:54. The work order's 14,946 is a mixture: its Chapter 1 figure of 2,693 is correct while the other four are the inflated ones.  
No allowance changed, because allowances derive from source words rather than script words. The counter used for all Phase B work is CAMERA\_V2/tools/spoken-count.awk, which counts only blockquote tokens containing a letter or a digit — so dashes, standalone punctuation and markup can never be counted as words again.  
Every Day 1 chapter is currently over its allowance, as Section 4 anticipates. Phase B does the cutting.

Fair — I described it in the programme's own shorthand. Here it is plainly.  
How a chapter's budget is set. Each teaching day has 10,500 words of script, which at 125 words a minute is 84 minutes of talking. That total is split among the day's nine chapters in proportion to how long each chapter is in the book. A chapter that's 10% of the day's book text gets 10% of the day's spoken words. So Chapter 56's budget is the answer to "how big is Chapter 56 in the book, compared with the other eight?"  
What I found. Chapter 56 measures 2,037 words in the book. 54 of those words are not teaching. They're a leftover note from whoever commissioned the writing, addressed to the writer, sitting in the middle of the chapter: "Keep this section consistent with the Chapter Contract's standing rule that AI is a workflow tool within your content system, not the subject or centerpiece of it…". It's an instruction about how to write the section, not the section. So the chapter's real size is 1,983 words.  
Why that doesn't fix itself. The formula divides each chapter's size by the day's total. If Chapter 56 shrinks by 54, the day's total shrinks too (16,919 → 16,865) — and because every share is "this chapter ÷ the total", a smaller total makes everyone else's share slightly bigger. So correcting one number quietly moves all nine.  
The three ways to handle it, and what each actually costs you:

|  | What happens | The catch |
| :---- | :---- | :---- |
| A. Shrink only Chapter 56 | its budget drops 1,265 → 1,231 words | the nine budgets then add to 10,466, not 10,500 — the day can only ever speak 16 seconds less than its ceiling |
| B. Re-split the whole day | every budget moves a few words so the nine still add to exactly 10,500; Chapter 56 ends at 1,233 | three chapters are already written and were judged against the old numbers — Chapter 31 would go 1,349→1,353, Chapter 33 696→699, Chapter 43 1,569→1,574, so their recorded margins stop matching their tables unless those are rewritten too |
| C. Note it, change nothing | Chapter 56 keeps 1,265 words | its budget stays about 34 words (16 seconds) more generous than the formula would give, and the record says exactly why |

The size of the stakes. The largest difference between these options is 34 words — about sixteen seconds inside an eighty-four-minute day. Nothing breaks under any of them.  
What I'd do: C. The programme already has this precedent — when Day 4's source total was corrected, the recomputed budgets were printed beside the issued ones rather than swapped in, because the issued figures are what the chapters were written and judged against. B is only defensible because Chapter 56 hasn't been written yet; but it would retroactively change three finished chapters' acceptance criteria, which is a bigger change than a 54-word defect justifies. If you do want the budget corrected, take B rather than A — A leaves the day's budgets failing to add up.  
So the decision isn't whether the 54 words are wrong (they are). It's: when a source measurement turns out to be wrong, does the budget get corrected, or does the record note it and the budget stand?  
The two other items are the same kind of decision, so you may as well settle all three at once. Chapter 14's twelve sections are plain bold lines in the book where every other chapter's are proper headings — it affects no script, since the Chapter 14 script already matches them by name; the choice is to leave it and record the exception, or make them headings like the other 51 chapters. And the stray block at line 11056 is a paste accident inside Chapter 39: a heading for Chapter 51, Chapter 51's seven section titles with no text beneath them, an empty heading, two notes to the writer, and a copy of §50.10 — Chapter 51 itself is intact elsewhere. Your earlier instruction was "report it and decide later", so this is the later.  
Rulings noted — all three are actionable. Let me start with the book repair, since it changes a hashed artefact. First, confirming exactly what will be converted:
